        ### A.3 The dynamics of gradient rescaling
        
        We found the denominator of gradient rescaling not to be subject to much variation, whereas the batch gradient norm dynamically changes the learning rate over time. [Figure 10.8](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_fig_008) compares the evolution of ∥∇[*t*]∥ for two different radii of the *k*\-support norm ball (with fixed *k*), where we compare to usual SGD training with weight decay. For both SGD and SFW, ∥∇[*t*]∥ is subject to noise and increases until 75 % of the training process, despite the continuous decrease of the train loss. In fact, the batch gradient norm is not significantly smaller than at the start of training even though the loss converges. This behavior might best be explainable by the presence of *batch-normalization* layers, whose interplay with weight decay has been analyzed by van Laarhoven \[[67](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_ref_067)\] and Hoffer et al. \[[31](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_ref_031)\]: layers preceding a batch-normalization are rescaling invariant, that is, their output remains unchanged when multiplying all parameters by a scalar; however, rescaling them results in inverse rescaling of the gradient norm in subsequent layers and iterations. Weight decay continuously decreases the scale of the parameters, and hence increases the scale of the batch gradient, where stronger decay of the former leads to stronger increase of the latter. Since in gradient rescaling the norm of the batch gradient also influences the strength of the decay of the parameters, this process has a self-accelerating dynamic. This dynamic results in larger steps toward the (sparse) vertices of the *k*\-support norm ball, leading to a stronger decay on the previous parameter configuration, which in turn increases the robustness to pruning, making gradient rescaling the method of choice in that setting.
        
        ![Figure 10.8 ResNet-18 on CIFAR-10: The evolution of the batch gradient norm when training SFW for different values of k and SGD for two weight decay strengths. The metric is averaged with respect to two random seeds and over all iterations within one epoch.](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-010_fig_008.jpg)
        
        Figure 10.8 ResNet-18 on CIFAR-10: The evolution of the batch gradient norm when training SFW for different values of k and SGD for two weight decay strengths. The metric is averaged with respect to two random seeds and over all iterations within one epoch.
        
        ### A.4 Proofs of LMO constructions
        
        In the following, we state the missing proof of the *k*\-support norm LMO (being a special case of the group-*k*\-support norm) and Lemma [10.1](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_stat_001).
        
        #### Lemma 10.3.
        
        *Given* ∇[*t*]*, let* *v*[*t*] ∈ 𝒞[*k*](*τ*) = *c**o**n**v*{*v* ∣ ∥*v*∥[0] ≤ *k*, ∥*v*∥[2] ≤ *τ*} *such that*
        
        $\\begin{matrix} {{\\lbrack\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rbrack}\_{\\mathit{i}} = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {- \\mathit{\\tau}{\\lbrack\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rbrack}\_{\\mathit{i}}/{\\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}^{{top}\_{\\mathit{k}}}\\|}\_{2}\\quad} & {\\textit{if}\\mspace{450mu}\\mathit{i} \\in {top}\_{\\mathit{k}}(|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}|),} \\\\ {0\\quad} & {\\textit{otherwise},} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        *where* ∇[*t*]^(*t**o**p*[*k*]) *is the vector obtained by setting to zero all* *n* − *k* *entries* \[∇[*t*]\][*j*] *of* ∇[*t*] *with* *j* ∉ *t**o**p*[*k*](|∇[*t*]|)*. Then* $\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\in \\operatorname{arg\\, min}\_{\\mathit{v} \\in \\mathcal{C}\_{\\mathit{k}}(\\mathit{\\tau})}\\langle\\mathit{v},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rangle$ *is a solution to Equation* ([10.2](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_002))*.*
        
        #### Proof.
        
        By construction, all vertices *v* of 𝒞[*k*](*τ*) satisfy ∥*v*∥[2] = *τ* and are *k*\-Sparse, that is, ∥*v*∥[0] ≤ *k*. Note that being *k*\-Sparse includes cases where more than *n* − *k* entries are zero. The minimum of Equation ([10.2](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_002)) is attained at one such *v*. Further recall the following reformulation of the euclidean inner product:
        
        (10.8)$\\begin{matrix} {\\langle\\mathit{v},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rangle = {\\|\\mathit{v}\\|}\_{2}{\\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|}\_{2}\\cos\\left( \\angle(\\mathit{v},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right) = \\mathit{\\tau}{\\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|}\_{2}\\cos\\left( \\angle(\\mathit{v},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        where ∠(*v*, ∇[*t*]) denotes the angle between *v* and ∇[*t*]. This term is minimized as soon as the angle between *v* and ∇[*t*] is maximal. If *v* was not required to be *k*\-Sparse, that is, *v* would be allowed to lie anywhere on the border of *B*[2](*τ*), the solution would clearly be given by  − *τ*∇[*t*]/∥∇[*t*]∥[2]. However, since *v* is *k*\-Sparse, the vector maximizing the angle to ∇[*t*] is the one that is closest to  − *τ*∇[*t*]/∥∇[*t*]∥[2] but is *k*\-Sparse at the same time. This is exactly the one claimed.  □
        
        #### Lemma 10.4.
        
        *Given* ∇[*t*] ∈ ℝ^(*n* × *m*)*, let* 𝒲[*t*] ∈ 𝒞[*k*]^(*σ*)(*τ*) *such that*
        
        $\\begin{matrix} {\\mathcal{W}\_{\\mathit{t}} = \\frac{- \\mathit{\\tau}}{{\\|\\mathit{\\sigma}(\\Sigma\_{\\mathit{k}})\\|}\_{2}}\\mathit{U}\_{\\mathit{k}}\\Sigma\_{\\mathit{k}}\\mathit{V}\_{\\mathit{k}}^{\\mathit{T}},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        *where* *U*[*k*]*Σ*[*k*]*V*[*k*]^(*T*) *is the truncated SVD of* ∇[*t*] *such that only the k largest singular values are kept. Then* $\\mathcal{W}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\in \\operatorname{arg\\, min}\_{\\mathit{v} \\in \\mathcal{C}\_{\\mathit{k}}^{\\mathit{\\sigma}}(\\mathit{\\tau})}\\langle\\mathit{v},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rangle$ *is a solution to Equation* ([10.2](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_002))*.*
        
        #### Proof.
        
        Recall that
        
        $\\begin{matrix} {\\mathcal{C}\_{\\mathit{k}}^{\\mathit{\\sigma}}(\\mathit{\\tau}) = {conv}\\left\\{ \\mathcal{W} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{\\mathit{n} \\times \\mathit{m}} \\mid \\text{rank}(\\mathcal{W}) \\leq \\mathit{k},\\left\\| \\mathit{\\sigma}(\\mathcal{W}) \\right\\|\_{2} \\leq \\mathit{\\tau} \\right\\}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Let 𝒲 be some minimizer. Note that rescaling a matrix by a scalar has no effect on its rank. Let us hence assume that ∥*σ*(𝒲)∥[2] = *α* for some *α* > 0 and characterize $\\mathcal{W} \\in \\operatorname{arg\\, min}\_{\\text{rank}(\\mathit{v}) \\leq \\mathit{k}}\\langle\\mathit{v},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rangle$. Again, we have
        
        (10.9)$\\begin{matrix} {{\\langle\\mathcal{W},\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\rangle}\_{\\mathit{F}} = \\left\\langle \\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathcal{W}},\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}} \\right\\rangle\_{2} = \\mathit{\\alpha}\\left\\| \\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}} \\right\\|\_{2}\\cos\\left( \\angle\\left( \\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathcal{W}},\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}} \\right) \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        where $\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathit{x}}$ is there vectorized form of matrix *x*. Since we can choose *α* ≤ *τ*, this term is minimal as soon as the angle $\\angle(\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathcal{W}},\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}})$ is maximal, that is, $\\cos(\\angle(\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathcal{W}},\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}})) < 0$ and *α* = *τ*, where we use the same Euclidean-geometric interpretation as in the proof for Lemma [10.3](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_stat_003) above. To obtain a maximal angle, we hence minimize the *l*[2]\-distance between $- \\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathcal{W}}$ and $\\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}}$ in compliance with the rank constraint. Since again ${\\| - \\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\mathcal{W}} - \\overset{\\leftarrow}{\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}}\\|}\_{2} = {\\| - \\mathcal{W} - \\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|}\_{\\mathit{F}}$, the Eckart–Young–Mirsky theorem yields the claim, where we rescale appropriately to meet the Schattennorm constraint.  □
        
        ### A.5 Convergence of SFW with gradient rescaling
        
        Before proving the convergence of SFW with gradient rescaling as stated informally in Theorem [10.2](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_stat_002), we first recall some central definitions and assumptions.
        
        #### A.5.1 Setting
        
        Let Ω be the set of training datapoints from which we sample uniformly at random. In Equation ([10.1](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_001)), we defined a unique loss function ℓ[*i*] for each datapoint. In the following, let ℓ(*θ*, *ω*[*i*]) = ℓ[*i*](*θ*) for *ω*[*i*] ∈ *Ω*. Similar to Reddi et al. \[[60](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_ref_060)\] and Pokutta et al. \[[57](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_ref_057)\], we define the SFW algorithm as in [Algorithm 10.1](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_fig_009), where the output *θ*[*a*] is chosen uniformly at random from all iterates *θ*[0], …, *θ*[*T* − 1].
        
        ![Algorithm 10.1 Stochastic Frank–Wolfe (SFW).](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-010_fig_009.jpg)
        
        Algorithm 10.1 Stochastic Frank–Wolfe (SFW).
        
        Let us recall some definitions. We denote the globally optimal solution by *θ*^⋆ and the *Frank–Wolfe Gap* at *θ* as
        
        (10.10)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}) = \\max\\limits\_{\\mathit{v} \\in \\mathcal{C}}\\left\\langle \\mathit{v} - \\mathit{\\theta}, - \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}) \\right\\rangle.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        We will use the same assumptions as Reddi et al. \[[60](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_ref_060)\]. First of all, let us assume that *L* is *M*\-smooth, that is,
        
        (10.11)$\\begin{matrix} {\\left\\| \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{x}) - \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\,\\mathit{y}) \\right\\| \\leq \\mathit{M}\\|\\mathit{x} - \\mathit{y}\\|} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        for all *x*, *y* ∈ 𝒞, which implies the well-known inequality
        
        (10.12)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{x}) \\leq \\mathit{L}(\\,\\mathit{y}) + \\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\,\\mathit{y}),\\mathit{x} - \\mathit{y} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}}{2}\\|\\mathit{x} - \\mathit{y}\\|^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Further, we assume the function *ℓ* to be *G*\-Lipschitz, that is, for all *x* ∈ 𝒞 and *ω* ∈ *Ω* we have
        
        (10.13)$\\begin{matrix} {\\left\\| \\nabla\\ell(\\mathit{x},\\mathit{\\omega}) \\right\\| \\leq \\mathit{G}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        A direct consequence is that the norm of the gradient estimator can be bounded as $\\|\\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}})\\| \\leq \\mathit{G}$.
        
        #### A.5.2 Convergence proof
        
        The following well-established lemma quantifies how closely $\\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta})$ approximates ∇*L*(*θ*). A proof can be found in Reddi et al. \[[60](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_ref_060)\].
        
        ##### Lemma 10.5.
        
        *Let* *ω*[1], …, *ω*[*b*] *be i. i. d. samples in* Ω*,* *θ* ∈ 𝒞*, and* $\\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}) = \\frac{1}{\\mathit{b}}\\sum\_{\\mathit{j} = 1}^{\\mathit{b}}\\nabla\\ell(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}},\\mathit{\\omega}\_{\\mathit{j}})$*. If ℓ is G-Lipschitz, then*
        
        (10.14)$\\begin{matrix} {\\left. \\mathbb{E} \\right\\|\\left. \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}) - \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}) \\right\\| \\leq \\frac{\\mathit{G}}{\\mathit{b}^{1/2}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        In the following, we denote the gradient estimator at iteration *t* as $\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}: = \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}})$ and the *l*[2]\-diameter 𝒟(𝒞) as 𝒟. Let *β* ∈ ℝ satisfy
        
        (10.15)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{\\beta} \\geq \\frac{2\\mathit{h}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0})}{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        for some given initialization *θ*[0] ∈ 𝒞 of the parameters, where *h*(*θ*[0]) = *L*(*θ*[0]) − *L*(*θ*^⋆) denotes the optimality gap of *θ*[0].
        
        Theorem 10.6. *For all* 0 ≤ *t* < *T**, let* *b*[*t*] = *b* = *T* *and* *η*[*t*] = ∥∇[*t*]∥*η* *where* $\\mathit{\\eta} = {(\\frac{\\mathit{h}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0})}{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\beta}})}^{1/2}$*. If* *θ*[*a*] *is chosen uniformly at random from the SFW iterates* {*θ*[*i*] : 0 ≤ *i* < *T*}*, then we have* $\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbb{E}\\,\\left\\lbrack \\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{a}}) \\cdot \\right\\|\\left. \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{a}}) \\right\\|\\rbrack \\leq \\frac{\\mathit{D}}{\\sqrt{\\mathit{T}}}\\left( \\sqrt{\\mathit{h}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0})\\mathit{M}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\beta}} + \\mathit{G}^{2} + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{G}\\mathit{D}}{2\\sqrt{2}} \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ *where* 𝔼 *denotes the expectation w. r. t. all the randomness present.* 
        
        ##### Proof.
        
        First of all, notice that *η*[*t*] is well-defined: Using *β* as defined above, we have
        
        (10.16)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{\\eta} \\leq \\left( \\frac{1}{2\\mathit{T}\\mathit{G}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2} = \\frac{1}{\\mathit{G}}\\frac{1}{\\sqrt{2\\mathit{T}}},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        and consequently, we obtain $\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}} = \\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|\\mathit{\\eta} \\leq \\frac{1}{\\sqrt{2\\mathit{T}}} \\leq 1$ by using that ∥∇[*t*]∥ ≤ *G*. By *M*\-smoothness of *L*, we have
        
        $\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1})} & {\\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}}{2}\\|\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Using the fact that *θ*[*t* + 1] = *θ*[*t*] + *η*[*t*](*v*[*t*] − *θ*[*t*]) and that ∥*v*[*t*] − *θ*[*t*]∥ ≤ *D*, it follows that
        
        (10.17)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1}) \\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}^{2}}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Now let
        
        (10.18)$\\begin{matrix} {{\\widehat{\\mathit{v}}}\_{\\mathit{t}} = \\operatorname{arg\\, min}\\limits\_{\\mathit{v} \\in \\mathcal{C}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v} \\right\\rangle = \\operatorname{arg\\, max}\\limits\_{\\mathit{v} \\in \\mathcal{C}}\\left\\langle - \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v} \\right\\rangle} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        be the LMO solution if we knew the exact gradient at iterate *θ*[*t*], where *t* = 0, …, *T* − 1. This minimizer is not part of the algorithm but is crucial in the subsequent analysis. Note that we have
        
        (10.19)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) = \\max\\limits\_{\\mathit{v} \\in \\mathcal{C}}\\left\\langle \\mathit{v} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}, - \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\rangle = \\left\\langle {\\widehat{\\mathit{v}}}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}, - \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\rangle.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Continuing from Equation ([10.17](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_026)), we therefore have
        
        $\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1})} & {\\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}^{2}}{2}} \\\\ & {\\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),{\\widehat{\\mathit{v}}}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}^{2}}{2}} \\\\ & {= \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),{\\widehat{\\mathit{v}}}\_{\\mathit{t}} - \\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} - {\\widehat{\\mathit{v}}}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}^{2}}{2}} \\\\ & {= \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\,\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\left\\langle \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}),\\mathit{v}\_{\\mathit{t}} - {\\widehat{\\mathit{v}}}\_{\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rangle + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}^{2}}{2},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        where the first inequality is just a reformulation of Equation ([10.17](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_026)) and the second one is due to the minimality of *v*[*t*]. Applying Cauchy–Schwarz and using the fact that the diameter of 𝒞 is *D*, we therefore have
        
        (10.20)$\\begin{matrix} {\\left. \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1}) \\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\,\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\mathit{D} \\right\\|\\left. \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\| + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}^{2}}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Now note that *η*[*t*] = ∥∇[*t*]∥*η* ≤ *G**η*, yielding
        
        (10.21)$\\begin{matrix} {\\left. \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1}) \\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\,\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) + \\mathit{\\eta}\\mathit{G}\\mathit{D} \\right\\|\\left. \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\widetilde{\\nabla}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\| + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}^{2}}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Let *θ*[0 : *t*] denote the sequence *θ*[0], …, *θ*[*t*]. Taking expectations and applying Lemma [10.5](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_stat_005), we get
        
        (10.22)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t} + 1}) \\leq \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) - \\,\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\left\\lbrack \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\rbrack + \\frac{\\mathit{D}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}}{\\mathit{b}^{1/2}} + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}^{2}}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        By rearranging and summing over *t* = 0, …, *T* − 1, we get the upper bound
        
        (10.23)$\\begin{matrix} {\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{t} = 0}^{\\mathit{T} - 1}\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\left\\lbrack \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\rbrack} & {\\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0}) - \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{T}}}\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{T}}) + \\frac{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{D}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}}{\\mathit{b}^{1/2}} + \\frac{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}^{2}}{2}} \\\\ & {\\leq \\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0}) - \\mathit{L}\\left( \\mathit{\\theta}^{\\star} \\right) + \\frac{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{D}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}}{\\mathit{b}^{1/2}} + \\frac{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}^{2}}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Now fix *t* and apply the law of total expectation to reformulate
        
        (10.24)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\left\\lbrack \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\rbrack = \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}}}\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\left\\lbrack \\mathit{\\eta}\_{\\mathit{t}}\\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\mid \\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rbrack = \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}}}\\left\\lbrack \\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}})\\mathit{\\eta} \\cdot \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\left\\lbrack \\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|\\mspace{430mu}|\\mspace{430mu}\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rbrack \\right\\rbrack,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        where we exploited that once *θ*[0 : *t*] is available, 𝒢(*θ*[*t*]) is not subject to randomness anymore. The expected norm of the gradient estimator given *θ*[*t*] depends only on the uniform selection of samples, allowing us to exploit the unbiasedness of the estimator as well as the convexity of the norm ‖·‖ using Jensen’s inequality as follows:
        
        (10.25)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t} + 1}}\\left\\lbrack \\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\|\\mspace{430mu}|\\mspace{430mu}\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rbrack} & {= \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\omega}}\\left\\lbrack \\|\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}}\\| \\mid \\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}} \\right\\rbrack} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (10.26)$\\begin{matrix} & {\\left. \\geq \\right\\|\\left. \\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\omega}}\\lbrack\\nabla\_{\\mathit{t}} \\mid \\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}}\\rbrack \\right\\|} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (10.27)$\\begin{matrix} & {\\left. = \\right\\|\\left. \\frac{1}{\\mathit{b}}\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{j} = 1}^{\\mathit{b}}\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\omega}\_{\\mathit{j}}}\\nabla\\ell(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}},\\mathit{\\omega}\_{\\mathit{j}}) \\right\\|} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (10.28)$\\begin{matrix} & {\\left. = \\right\\|\\left. \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\|.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Combining this with Equation ([10.23](#9783111375854_057097.xhtml#b_9783111376776-010_eq_033)), we obtain
        
        (10.29)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathit{\\eta}\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{t} = 0}^{\\mathit{T} - 1}\\mathbb{E}\_{\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0:\\mathit{t}}}\\left\\lbrack \\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\cdot \\right\\|\\left. \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{t}}) \\right\\|\\rbrack \\leq \\mathit{h}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0}) + \\frac{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{D}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}}{\\mathit{b}^{1/2}} + \\frac{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}^{2}}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        Using the definition of *θ*[*a*], being a uniformly at random chosen iterate from *θ*[0], …, *θ*[*T* − 1], we conclude the proof with the following inequality:
        
        (10.30)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbb{E}\\,\\left\\lbrack \\mathcal{G}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{a}}) \\cdot \\right\\|\\left. \\nabla\\mathit{L}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{\\mathit{a}}) \\right\\|\\rbrack} & {\\leq \\frac{\\mathit{h}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0})}{\\mathit{T}\\mathit{\\eta}} + \\frac{\\mathit{D}\\mathit{G}^{2}}{\\mathit{b}^{1/2}} + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{D}^{2}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\eta}}{2}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (10.31)$\\begin{matrix} & {\\leq \\frac{\\mathit{D}}{\\sqrt{\\mathit{T}}}\\left( \\sqrt{\\mathit{h}(\\mathit{\\theta}\_{0})\\mathit{M}\\mathit{G}^{2}\\mathit{\\beta}} + \\mathit{G}^{2} + \\frac{\\mathit{M}\\mathit{G}\\mathit{D}}{2\\sqrt{2}} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
         □

        # Approximation of generalized frequency response functions via vector fitting
        
        Antonio Carlucci Ion Victor Gosea Stefano Grivet-Talocia 
        
        #### Abstract
        
        This paper describes a method for data-driven approximation of nonlinear systems, using the notion of generalized frequency response functions as provided by the Volterra series theory. Using rational approximation, frequency-domain input–output data are fitted to a bilinear model structure. The proposed algorithm performs optimization of model coefficients through a greedy approach that relies on linear least-squares solves only, thus ensuring speed and scalability.
        
        Keywords:  Volterra series,  bilinear systems,  vector fitting algorithm,  generalized frequency response functions,  least-squares fitting,  Burgers’ equation,  MSC 2020:  41A20,  93C10,  93C80,  
        
        ## 1 Introduction and background
        
        The Volterra series theory for nonlinear systems \[[21](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_021)\], \[[20](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_020)\] is a modeling approach based on expanding a given time-invariant input–output map *y*(*t*) = *G*\[*u*(*t*)\] using a series of generalized time-domain convolutions as
        
        (11.1)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{y}(\\mathit{t}) = \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\infty}\\int\\limits\_{0}^{\\mathit{t}}\\mathit{h}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}})\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t} - \\mathit{\\tau}\_{1})\\cdots\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t} - \\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}})\\text{d}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1}\\ldots\\text{d}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        where *u*(*t*) is the system’s input (vanishing for *t* < 0), while *y*(*t*) is the output. This paper focuses on single-input, single-output (SISO) systems, that is, *u*(*t*), *y*(*t*) ∈ ℝ are scalars. In ([11.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_001)), the output is decomposed as a sum of contributions *y*[*m*](*t*) from *homogeneous* subsystems, whose response is given as a multidimensional convolution with the one-sided kernel *h*[*m*](*τ*[1], …, *τ*[*m*]). This series expansion is typically used to model mildly nonlinear systems since it has local validity around an operating point (in this case, *u*(*t*) = 0) and it is ensured to be convergent if the amplitude of *u*(*t*) is sufficiently small (see \[[20](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_020)\], \[[5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_005)\] for details). Equation ([11.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_001)) is not enough to uniquely define the kernels *h*[*m*], since different choices can describe the same input–output relation. Requiring that the *h*[*m*]’s are symmetric, that is, invariant under arbitrary permutations of the arguments, leads to a unique definition that is particularly convenient because they can be easily obtained analytically and measured from simulations. This formalism is valuable from a modeling standpoint because it generalizes the well-known convolution formula of linear systems, and allows defining generalized frequency response functions (GFRFs) through the multivariate Laplace transform \[[10](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_010)\]
        
        (11.2)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{H}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{s}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}) = \\int\\limits\_{0}^{\\infty}\\!\\cdots\\int\\limits\_{0}^{\\infty}\\mathit{h}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}})\\mathit{e}^{- \\sum\_{\\mathit{i} = 1}^{\\mathit{m}}\\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{i}}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{i}}}\\text{d}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1}\\ldots\\text{d}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        where *h*[*m*](*τ*[1], …, *τ*[*m*]) is the symmetric kernel and *H*[*m*](*s*[1], …, *s*[*m*]) is also called the degree-*m* symmetric transfer function.
        
        The objective of this paper is to derive a dynamical model starting from sampled data of symmetric transfer functions *H*[*m*](*s*[1], …, *s*[*m*]). To this aim, a particularly convenient choice of the model structure is that of bilinear systems. These can be represented with the following state-space form:
        
        (11.3)$\\begin{matrix} {\\overset{˙}{\\mathit{x}}(\\mathit{t}) = \\mathit{A}\\mathit{x}(\\mathit{t}) + \\mathit{N}\\mathit{x}(\\mathit{t})\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t}) + \\mathit{B}\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t}),\\quad\\mathit{y}(\\mathit{t}) = \\mathit{C}\\mathit{x}(\\mathit{t}).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Such systems can be also viewed as time-varying linear systems, by rewriting the state equation as $\\overset{˙}{\\mathit{x}}(\\mathit{t}) = \\bar{\\mathit{A}}(\\mathit{t})\\mathit{x}(\\mathit{t}) + \\mathit{B}\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t})$ with $\\bar{\\mathit{A}}(\\mathit{t}) = \\mathit{A} + \\mathit{N}\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t})$. Therefore, stability and other relevant properties can be deduced from the already existing theory for linear systems, and hence, the bilinear structure has proven very useful and tractable for modeling. It is also sufficiently general since a broad class of systems with analytic nonlinearities can be embedded into a bilinear representation, for example, through the Carleman linearization approach \[[7](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_007)\] as in \[[6](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_006)\]. Additionally, by imposing specific boundary conditions for PDEs as in \[[3](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_003)\], bilinear models can be derived after semidiscretization in the spatial domain. For bilinear systems, several model reduction (MOR) techniques have been described as an immediate extension of their linear counterpart. Intrusive methods for MOR of bilinear systems, mostly based on moment matching (rational Krylov approaches) or balancing, were proposed in \[[6](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_006)\], \[[3](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_003)\] (see also the references therein), together with optimality-enforced methods and matching of infinite series \[[23](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_023)\], \[[2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_002)\], \[[13](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_013)\]. Then data-driven approaches were also proposed, starting with a direct extension of the Loewner framework to bilinear systems in \[[1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_001)\], and with more recent work in \[[16](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_016)\] that uses time-domain data to infer values of (symmetric) transfer functions; the latter was extended to quadratic systems in \[[17](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_017)\]. Subspace identification techniques for bilinear systems were proposed in \[[12](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_012)\], while the problem of structure-preserving MOR was treated in \[[4](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_004)\]. This paper proposes a novel approach toward data-driven approximation of GFRFs based on rational fitting. We provide an extension of the vector fitting algorithm to the nonlinear setting, featuring a greedy strategy whereby a sequence of linear least-squares problems is solved to optimize the coefficients of a conveniently chosen bilinear model structure.
        
        This paper is organized as follows. The notation and the model structure are provided in Section [2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_002). The core ideas for fitting GFRFs are introduced in Section [2.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_003), followed by a discussion on the relation between the fitting error and the input–output response in Section [2.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_004). Then algorithmic tools for rational approximation based on Vector Fitting (VF) are reviewed and adapted in Section [2.3](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_005). Two numerical experiments are studied and the results are reported in Section [3](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_006), before concluding with Section [4](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_009).
        
        ## 2 Formulation
        
        In order to introduce the problem data, let us use the shorthand **s**[*m*] ≜ (*s*[1], …, *s*[*m*]) to denote a point in the *m*\-dimensional frequency space. The starting point for model inference is thus a data set ℋ = {ℋ[*m*]}[*m* = 1]^(*M*) with evaluations of degree-*m* transfer functions at an arbitrary set of points
        
        (11.4)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{H}\_{\\mathit{m}} = \\left\\{ \\left( \\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})},{\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right),\\,\\mathit{k} = 1,\\ldots,\\mathit{K}\_{\\mathit{m}} \\right\\},\\quad\\mathit{m} = 1,\\ldots,\\mathit{M}.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        This data is fitted with a predefined model structure corresponding to a particular bilinear dynamical system *G* with the following form:
        
        (11.5)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathit{G}:\\,\\left\\{ \\begin{array}{l} {{\\overset{˙}{\\mathit{x}}}\_{1}(\\mathit{t}) = \\mathit{A}\_{1}\\mathit{x}\_{1}(\\mathit{t}) + \\mathit{B}\_{1}\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t}),\\quad} \\\\ {{\\overset{˙}{\\mathit{x}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{t}) = \\mathit{A}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\mathit{x}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{t}) + \\mathit{N}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\mathit{x}\_{\\mathit{m} - 1}(\\mathit{t})\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t}),\\quad 2 \\leq \\mathit{m} \\leq \\mathit{M},\\quad} \\\\ {\\mathit{y}(\\mathit{t}) = \\sum\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\mathit{C}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\mathit{x}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{t}).\\quad} \\\\ \\end{array} \\right.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        This was introduced in \[[20](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_020)\] as a way to construct bilinear realizations of Volterra transfer functions. Its structure is such that all kernels beyond the first *M* are zero, implying that *G* is a *polynomial* system of degree *M* (i. e., the sum in ([11.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_001)) contains *M* terms). In addition, state equations corresponding to every individual *m* in ([11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_005)) correspond to the *m*th degree *homogeneous subsystem* of *G* \[[20](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_020)\], that is, the *m*th term in ([11.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_001)). The system ([11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_005)) can be interpreted as a cascade of linear systems with a multiplicative nonlinearity in between. Denoting with *H*[*m*]^((*k*)) the symmetric transfer functions of *G* evaluated at **s**[*m*]^((*k*)), the model coefficients should be optimized so that *H*[*m*] is close to ${\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}$, that is, $\\mathit{H}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\approx {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})}$.
        
        It is to be mentioned that *G* in ([11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_005)) could be recast into a standard bilinear system format as in ([11.3](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_003)). The way to do this is as suggested by the realization procedure in \[[20](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_020)\], that is, by putting together the bilinear system matrices (*A*, *B*, *C*, *N*), as follows:
        
        (11.6)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{A} = \\text{blkdiag}\\{\\mathit{A}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{A}\_{\\mathit{M}}\\},\\quad\\mathit{B} = \\text{col}\\{\\mathit{B}\_{1},0,\\ldots,0\\},} \\\\ {\\mathit{N} = \\begin{pmatrix} 0 & & & \\\\ \\mathit{N}\_{2} & 0 & & \\\\ & \\mathit{N}\_{3} & 0 & \\\\ & & \\ddots & \\ddots \\\\ \\end{pmatrix},\\quad\\mathit{C} = \\begin{pmatrix} \\mathit{C}\_{1} & \\cdots & \\mathit{C}\_{\\mathit{M}} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Different from \[[16](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_016)\], where the matrix *N* of the fitted bilinear system is computed to match the realization (*A*, *B*, *C*) computed with the Loewner framework in the first step, here we construct individual submodels (*A*[*m*], *N*[*m*], *C*[*m*]) at each step *m* ≥ 2. If necessary, these submodels may be assembled into a standard bilinear system, by following ([11.6](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_006)).
        
        ### 2.1 Fitting of symmetric transfer functions
        
        The symmetric transfer functions of ([11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_005)) can be expressed recursively. Starting from *m* = 1, let us define the auxiliary functions
        
        (11.7)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{X}\_{1}(\\mathit{s}\_{1})} & {= {(\\mathit{s}\_{1}\\mathit{I} - \\mathit{A}\_{1})}^{- 1}\\mathit{B}\_{1},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (11.8)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{X}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}})} & {= \\left\\lbrack (\\mathit{s}\_{1} + \\cdots + \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}})\\mathit{I} - \\mathit{A}\_{\\mathit{m}} \\right\\rbrack^{- 1}\\mathit{N}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\mathit{Q}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}),\\quad\\mathit{m} \\geq 2,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (11.9)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{Q}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}})} & {= \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{i} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\mathit{X}\_{\\mathit{m} - 1}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\backslash\\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{i}}),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        with **s**[*m*] ∖ *s*[*i*] ≜ (*s*[1], …, *s*[*i* − 1], *s*[*i* + 1], …, *s*[*m*]). The symmetric transfer functions become *H*[*m*](**s**[*m*]) = *C*[*m*]*X*[*m*](**s**[*m*]), with the explicit formulation below:
        
        (11.10)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathit{H}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}) = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{C}\_{1}{(\\mathit{s}\_{1}\\mathit{I} - \\mathit{A}\_{1})}^{- 1}\\mathit{B}\_{1},\\quad} & {\\mathit{m} = 1,} \\\\ {\\frac{1}{\\mathit{m}!}\\mathit{C}\_{\\mathit{m}}{\\lbrack(\\mathit{s}\_{1} + \\cdots + \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}})\\mathit{I} - \\mathit{A}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\rbrack}^{- 1}\\mathit{N}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\mathit{Q}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}),\\quad} & {\\mathit{m} \\geq 2.} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        Expressions ([11.9](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_009))–([11.10](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_010)) can be easily derived for the assumed system structure ([11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_005)) by applying the so-called growing exponential approach; see \[[20](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_020)\].
        
        In principle, the approximation problem we need to solve is to find model parameters so that the following cost function is minimized:
        
        (11.11)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{J} = \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{k} = 1}^{\\mathit{K}\_{\\mathit{m}}}\\left| \\mathit{H}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right) - {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right) \\right|^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        Our approach is to greedily optimize individual terms of the summation corresponding to each *m*. Starting from *m* = 1, we consider the approximation
        
        (11.12)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathit{C}\_{1}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{1}^{(\\mathit{k})}\\mathit{I} - \\mathit{A}\_{1} \\right)^{- 1}\\mathit{B}\_{1} \\approx {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{1}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{1}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right),\\quad\\mathit{k} = 1,\\ldots,\\mathit{K}\_{1},} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        that is equivalent to fitting a univariate rational function to given data, a problem known as *rational fitting* and efficiently solvable using existing algorithms, such as AAA \[[19](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_019)\] or vector fitting \[[15](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_015)\].
        
        As for *m* > 1, the recursive expression in ([11.10](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_010)) clearly shows the main idea of our formulation, based on observing that the *m*th degree transfer function is the product of a univariate transfer function
        
        (11.13)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{F}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{s}) = \\frac{1}{\\mathit{m}!}\\mathit{C}\_{\\mathit{m}}{(\\mathit{s}\\mathit{I} - \\mathit{A}\_{\\mathit{m}})}^{- 1}\\mathit{N}\_{\\mathit{m}},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        evaluated at *s* = *s*[1] + ⋯ + *s*[*m*] with the function *Q*[*m*](**s**[*m*]), which only depends on lower-degree subsystems. This implies that, if the system coefficients *A*[1], …, *A*[*m*] and *B*[1], *N*[2], …, *N*[*m*] are known up to index *m*, the quantity *Q*[*m*](**s**[*m*]) is entirely determined. Therefore, the approximation problem is reduced to optimizing the coefficients of *F*[*m*](*s*) for each higher-degree index *m* = 2, …, *M* so that
        
        (11.14)$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{F}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{1}^{(\\mathit{k})} + \\cdots + \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right)\\mathit{Q}\\left( \\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right) \\approx {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\left( \\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right),\\quad\\mathit{k} = 1,\\ldots,\\mathit{K}\_{\\mathit{m}},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 
        
        which can be tackled again with rational fitting algorithms. The greedy aspect of this method lies in considering *Q*(**s**[*m*]^((*k*))) a given constant as resulting from optimization of previous terms of the summation ([11.11](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_011)), rather than jointly optimizing all system parameters in ([11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_005)) simultaneously.
        
        ### 2.2 Cost function and model error
        
        The motivation for using *J* in ([11.11](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_011)) as a measure of model mismatch is that it works as a proxy for the *H*[2] norm of the error system. In particular, using the notation ${\\breve{\\mathit{h}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{n}})$ for the *true* Volterra kernels of the original system and *h*[*m*](*τ*[1], …, *τ*[*n*]) for the fitted degree-*M* model, we can define the error $\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{n}}) = \\mathit{h}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{n}}) - {\\breve{\\mathit{h}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{n}})$. Following the definition in \[[23](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_023)\], \[[13](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_013)\], the *H*[2] norm of the error system *E* would be
        
        (11.15)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\|\\mathit{E}\\|\_{\\mathit{H}\_{2}} = \\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\infty}\\|\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\|\_{2}^{2}},\\quad\\|\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\|\_{2}^{2} = \\int\\limits\_{0}^{\\infty}\\cdots\\int\\limits\_{0}^{\\infty}\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}{(\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}})}^{2}\\mathit{d}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{1}\\cdots\\mathit{d}\\mathit{\\tau}\_{\\mathit{m}}.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        Considering the magnitude of the output error $\\mathit{e}(\\mathit{t}) = \\breve{\\mathit{y}}(\\mathit{t}) - \\mathit{y}(\\mathit{t})$,
        
        (11.16)$\\begin{array}{r} \\left| \\mathit{e}(\\mathit{t}) \\middle| \\leq \\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\|\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\|\_{2}^{2}}\\sqrt{\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\|\\mathit{u}\\|\_{2}^{2\\mathit{m}}} + \\middle| \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = \\mathit{M} + 1}^{\\infty}{\\breve{\\mathit{y}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{t}) \\middle| . \\right. \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        This estimate shows that the error contains two components, the first one depends on the modeling error of the first *M* kernels, while the second is the unmodeled nonlinearity of degree higher than *M*. The connection with the cost function *J* is made through Plancherel’s theorem to express ∥*e*[*m*]∥[2]² in terms of the *H*[2]\-norm of $\\mathit{E}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}) = \\mathit{H}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}) - {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}})$,
        
        (11.17)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\|\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\|\_{2}^{2} = \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\frac{1}{{(2\\mathit{\\pi})}^{\\mathit{m}}}\\int\\limits\_{- \\infty}^{\\infty}\\cdots\\int\\limits\_{- \\infty}^{\\infty}\\left| \\mathit{E}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{j}\\mathit{\\omega}\_{1},\\ldots,\\mathit{j}\\mathit{\\omega}\_{\\mathit{m}}) \\right|^{2}\\mathit{d}\\mathit{\\omega}\_{1}\\ldots\\mathit{d}\\mathit{\\omega}\_{\\mathit{m}},} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        and *J* can be viewed as a discrete analogue of the frequency-domain integrals.
        
        As for the choice of *M*, Equation ([11.16](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_016)) suggests that it should be chosen as the smallest degree for which the error due to the higher-degree responses ( *m* ≥ *M* + 1) are cumulatively negligible compared to the modeling error $\\sum\_{\\mathit{m} = 1}^{\\mathit{M}}\\|\\mathit{e}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\|^{2}$ arising from the fitting procedure.
        
        ### 2.3 Weighted vector fitting for GFRFs
        
        This section completes the formulation by describing the main enabling tool to solve the approximation problems ([11.14](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_014)) and ([11.12](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_012)), that is, the weighted vector fitting algorithm. In particular, ([11.12](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_012)) can be tackled with the standard formulation of VF \[[15](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_015)\], \[[11](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_011)\], \[[14](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_014)\]. On the other hand, ([11.14](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_014)) requires incorporating the arbitrary but known weighting vectors *Q*(**s**[*m*]^((*k*))) using a modified formulation such as \[[8](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_008)\], here reviewed for the specific problem at hand. First of all, consider problem ([11.14](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_014)) for a particular *m*, restated as
        
        (11.18)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\text{minimize}\\,\\,\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{k} = 1}^{\\mathit{K}}\\left| \\mathit{F}\_{\\mathit{m}}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{1}^{(\\mathit{k})} + \\cdots + \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right)\\mathit{Q}\\left( \\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right) - {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right|^{2},} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        where *F*[*m*](*s*) is unknown and represented in pole-residue form as
        
        (11.19)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathit{F}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{s}) = \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{i} = 1}^{\\mathit{\\nu}}\\frac{\\mathit{R}\_{\\mathit{i}}}{\\mathit{s} - \\mathit{p}\_{\\mathit{i}}},} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        with the index *m* being omitted in both the *R*[*i*]’s and also the *p*[*i*]’s. Note that this pole-residue expansion is another way of parameterizing *F*[*m*](*s*) through coefficients *R*[*i*] and *p*[*i*], instead of the coefficients *C*[*m*], *A*[*m*], *N*[*m*] used in ([11.13](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_013)). However, it is always possible to write *F*[*m*](*s*) in the form ([11.13](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_013)) starting from the pole-residue ([11.19](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_019)), through a *realization* procedure (see \[[14](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_014)\]).
        
        Going back to the optimization problem in ([11.18](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_018)), a key observation is that if *p*[*i*] are given constants, ([11.18](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_018)) is an easily solvable linear least-squares (LS) problem in the variables *R*[*i*]. However, if *p*[*i*] are to be optimized, this is no longer true and the problem becomes much harder. For this reason, VF goes through a preliminary pole identification phase to find poles *p*[*i*] through an iterated solution of a simplified problem. In this stage, a double barycentric form is used to represent *F*[*m*](*s*) as
        
        (11.20)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathit{F}\_{\\mathit{m}}(\\mathit{s}) = \\frac{\\mathit{N}(\\mathit{s})}{\\mathit{d}(\\mathit{s})},\\quad\\mathit{N}(\\mathit{s}) = \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{i} = 1}^{\\mathit{\\nu}}\\frac{\\mathit{N}\_{\\mathit{i}}}{\\mathit{s} - \\mathit{q}\_{\\mathit{i}}},\\quad\\mathit{d}(\\mathit{s}) = 1 + \\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{i} = 1}^{\\mathit{\\nu}}\\frac{\\mathit{d}\_{\\mathit{i}}}{\\mathit{s} - \\mathit{q}\_{\\mathit{i}}},} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        using a set of auxiliary poles {*q*[*i*]}[*i* = 1]^(*ν*). Starting from an initial guess of *q*[*i*], this is updated through several iterations where the following modified cost function is minimized:
        
        (11.21)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\text{min}\\,\\,\\sum\\limits\_{\\mathit{k} = 1}^{\\mathit{K}}\\left| \\mathit{N}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{1}^{(\\mathit{k})} + \\cdots + \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right)\\mathit{Q}\\left( \\mathbf{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right) - {\\breve{\\mathit{H}}}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})}\\mathit{d}\\left( \\mathit{s}\_{1}^{(\\mathit{k})} + \\cdots + \\mathit{s}\_{\\mathit{m}}^{(\\mathit{k})} \\right) \\right|^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        Solving ([11.21](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_021)) at a given iteration yields coefficients *d*[*i*] of *d*(*s*), that are used to update the auxiliary poles for the next iteration through the rule *q*[*i*] ← zeros{*d*(*s*)}. In this way, ([11.21](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_021)) is optimized repeatedly with an updated choice of *q*[*i*] each time. Upon convergence or after a maximum number of iterations, the *q*[*i*]’s are chosen to be the model poles *p*[*i*] in ([11.19](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_019)). Finally, ([11.18](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_018)) and ([11.19](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_eq_019)) with fixed *p*[*i*] are solved via linear LS to find *R*[*i*].
        
        Convergence to a given set of poles *q*[*i*] corresponds to the condition *d*(*s*) → 1, detected either by checking whether the norm of coefficients $\\sum\_{\\mathit{i} = 1}^{\\mathit{\\nu}}\\mathit{d}\_{\\mathit{i}}^{2}$ falls below a predefined tolerance, or by looking at the maximum deviation |*d*(*s*) − 1|[∞]. Convergence of VF is discussed in \[[18](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_018)\], where it is shown that the algorithm might not converge in a strong sense. Nonetheless, VF still produces good solutions to the rational approximation problem, for which a globally optimal solution is hard to obtain due to the strongly nonconvex nature of the related optimization problem. In fact, thanks to its robustness and reliability, VF has become the method of choice in design automation tools and flows available on the market, in particular for electronic system design applications.
        
        The proposed algorithm for symmetric transfer function fitting is summarized as the pseudocode in [Algorithm 11.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_001).
        
        ![Algorithm 11.1 Approximation of GFRFs.](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_001.jpg)
        
        Algorithm 11.1 Approximation of GFRFs.
        
        ## 3 Numerical examples and discussion
        
        ### 3.1 Viscous Burgers’ equation
        
        This section reports on the example of the viscous Burgers’ equation
        
        (11.22)$\\begin{array}{r} {\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\mathit{t}}\\mathit{v}(\\mathit{x},\\mathit{t}) - \\mathit{\\nu}\\frac{\\partial^{2}}{\\partial\\mathit{x}^{2}}\\mathit{v}(\\mathit{x},\\mathit{t}) + \\mathit{v}(\\mathit{x},\\mathit{t})\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\mathit{x}}\\mathit{v}(\\mathit{x},\\mathit{t}) = 0,} \\\\ \\end{array}$ 
        
        describing the velocity *v*(*x*, *t*) of a fluid in one spatial coordinate. The viscosity parameter was set to *ν* = 0.02. By following \[[6](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_006)\], the PDE was semi-discretized in space through a finite difference scheme with 200 points. The resulting nonlinear dynamical system describing the temporal evolution was formulated as a quadratic-bilinear system of size *q* = 200.
        
        A data-driven bilinear model with degree *M* = 2 could be constructed by sampling the first- and second-degree transfer functions *H*[1](*s*[1]) and *H*[2](*s*[1], *s*[2]). In particular, *H*[1](*s*[1]) was sampled on the imaginary axis at *K*[1] = 200 points, log-spaced and corresponding to the frequency band \[10^( − 5), 800\] Hz. The function *H*[2](*s*[1], *s*[2]) was sampled at *K*[2] = 4964 points, with both *s*[1] and *s*[2] purely imaginary and in the frequency interval \[10^( − 5), 400\] Hz. Running the proposed algorithm with *ν* = 8 poles, 40 iterations, and *M* = 2 to approximate *H*[1] and *H*[2] leads to a fitted bilinear model with overall order 13. Frequency-domain accuracy is reported in [Figure 11.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_002), where the left panel shows the relative error with respect to both *s*[1] and *s*[2], and the right panel directly compares the responses.
        
        ![Figure 11.1 Error analysis for the example in Section 3.1. Left panel: Relative error on \\mathit{H}\_{2} for both \\mathit{s}\_{1} and \\mathit{s}\_{2} on the imaginary axis. Right panel: Model-data comparison for \\mathit{H}\_{2}(\\mathit{j}2\\mathit{\\pi}\\mathit{f}\_{1},\\mathit{j}2\\mathit{\\pi}\\mathit{f}\_{2}) with respect to \\mathit{f}\_{2} and for several fixed values of \\mathit{f}\_{1}.](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_002.jpg)
        
        Figure 11.1 Error analysis for the example in Section [3.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_007). *Left panel*: Relative error on *H*[2] for both *s*[1] and *s*[2] on the imaginary axis. *Right panel*: Model-data comparison for *H*[2](*j*2*π**f*[1], *j*2*π**f*[2]) with respect to *f*[2] and for several fixed values of *f*[1].
        
        In the time domain, the responses of the full-order model are compared with the fitted one in [Figure 11.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_003) (left panel). In this experiment, *t* ∈ \[0, 10\] s and the input is an amplitude-modulated chirp *u*(*t*) = 0.1cos \[2*π**f*[0](*t*)*t*\]\[1 − 1/2cos (2*π*2*t*)\], with *f*[0](*t*) sweeping from 1 mHz to 10 Hz, in order to test accuracy over a broad frequency range. In the right panel of [Figure 11.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_003), we also show that the system nonlinearity is being modeled correctly because adding the degree-2 subsystem leads to a substantial error reduction compared to a simple degree-1 approximation (where only *H*[1] is fitted).
        
        ![Figure 11.2 Time-domain solution of the example in Section 3.1. The left panel compares the full-order model response with that of the fitted model (of degree 2). The right panel reports the instantaneous error for two models with \\mathit{M} = 1,2, showing that the nonlinear ( \\mathit{M} = 2) model is more accurate than the linear approximation (\\mathit{M} = 1).](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_003.jpg)
        
        Figure 11.2 Time-domain solution of the example in Section [3.1](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_007). The *left panel* compares the full-order model response with that of the fitted model (of degree 2). The *right panel* reports the instantaneous error for two models with *M* = 1, 2, showing that the nonlinear ( *M* = 2) model is more accurate than the linear approximation (*M* = 1).
        
        ### 3.2 Nonlinear RC ladder
        
        This example is an electrical network with nonlinear elements, originally introduced in \[[9](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_009)\]. In particular, it is an RC ladder circuit with diodes in parallel to resistors (see [Figure 11.3](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_004)). The input *u*(*t*) is a current source, while the output *y*(*t*) is the voltage at the first node.
        
        ![Figure 11.3 Nonlinear electrical network of the example in Section 3.2, picture adapted from \[22\].](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_004.jpg)
        
        Figure 11.3 Nonlinear electrical network of the example in Section [3.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_008), picture adapted from \[[22](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_022)\].
        
        Diodes have an exponential characteristic so that the original state equation contains nonpolynomial nonlinearities. Therefore, starting from a model with *q* = 50 states, Carleman bilinearization was used to obtain a lifted bilinear model with *q*[bil] = 50² + 50. The resulting system is considered the starting point for applying the proposed data-driven modeling scheme.
        
        We sampled *H*[1](*s*[1]) at *K*[1] = 101 points on the imaginary axis *s*[1] = *j*2*π**f*[1], with *f*[1] ∈ \[10^( − 1), 10²\] in addition to *s*[1]^((1)) = 0. Regarding higher-order transfer functions, we randomly selected *K*[2] = 343 and *K*[3] = 101 points to sample *H*[2](*s*[1], *s*[2]) and *H*[3](*s*[1], *s*[2], *s*[3]).
        
        Using the proposed algorithm with *ν* = 6 poles and 20 iterations leads to the accurate fitting of the first *M* = 3 frequency responses, as shown in [Figure 11.4](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_005) for *m* = 2. In time-domain, the model was tested in a 2-s long simulation with amplitude-modulated chirp input $\\mathit{u}(\\mathit{t}) = 2 \\cdot \\sin\\lbrack 2\\mathit{\\pi}\\mathit{f}\_{0}(\\mathit{t})\\mathit{t} + \\mathit{\\pi}/2\\rbrack\\lbrack 1 - \\frac{1}{2}\\cos(2\\mathit{\\pi}\\mathit{t})\\rbrack$, with *f*[0](*t*) varying linearly from 0.5 Hz to 50 Hz.
        
        ![Figure 11.4 Error analysis for the example in Section 3.2. Left panel: Model-data comparison for the degree-1 transfer function. Right panel: Degree-2 transfer function with respect to \\mathit{f}\_{2} for several fixed values of \\mathit{f}\_{1}.](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_005.jpg)
        
        Figure 11.4 Error analysis for the example in Section [3.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_008). *Left panel*: Model-data comparison for the degree-1 transfer function. *Right panel*: Degree-2 transfer function with respect to *f*[2] for several fixed values of *f*[1].
        
        [Figure 11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_006) compares the original and reduced model responses, showing that adding higher-order transfer functions decreases the error and that the degree-3 model is sufficient to produce a response that is indistinguishable from the full-order model for this case. Note that the error curves depicted in [Figure 11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_006) are related to the time-domain simulation used for testing that involves an oscillatory signal. The input has here been chosen to push the system into a regime of nonlinear operation where the contribution from the higher-order transfer functions can be appreciated and, in particular, models with *M* = 1, 2 are insufficient, as shown in [Figure 11.6](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_007).
        
        ![Figure 11.5 Time-domain solution for the example in Section 3.2. Left panel: Comparison of full-order and fitted model of degree \\mathit{M} = 3. Right panel: Evolution of instantaneous error with respect to model degree.](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_006.jpg)
        
        Figure 11.5 Time-domain solution for the example in Section [3.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_008). *Left panel*: Comparison of full-order and fitted model of degree *M* = 3. *Right panel*: Evolution of instantaneous error with respect to model degree.
        
        ![Figure 11.6 Left panel: Time-domain response of example in Section 3.2, highlighting the qualitative difference between models with \\mathit{M} = 2 and \\mathit{M} = 3. Right panel: Error in the time-domain solution of Figure 11.5, measured as root mean square (RMS) and peak error (max. in \\mathit{t} \\in \\lbrack 0,2\\rbrack).](graphic/converted/b_9783111376776-011_fig_007.jpg)
        
        Figure 11.6 *Left panel*: Time-domain response of example in Section [3.2](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_s_008), highlighting the qualitative difference between models with *M* = 2 and *M* = 3. *Right panel*: Error in the time-domain solution of [Figure 11.5](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_fig_006), measured as root mean square (RMS) and peak error (max. in *t* ∈ \[0, 2\]).
        
        ## 4 Conclusions
        
        To summarize, this work described a new algorithm for data-driven approximation of nonlinear input–output maps in the frequency domain, based on Volterra series. The method uses samples of the symmetric transfer functions, directly measurable from response evaluation, without requiring access to the first-principles description of the system to be modeled. Compared to previous work \[[16](#9783111375854_071706.xhtml#b_9783111376776-011_ref_016)\], this framework is flexible as it can approximate GFRFs beyond the second degree. The entire procedure is fast and scalable as it consists of a sequence of linear least-squares optimization problems, thanks to a greedy approach that provides simplicity in exchange for exact optimality. Future work could address this issue, by investigating extensions of this algorithm in which transfer functions are fitted simultaneously rather than sequentially.
